<Task>Produce 10 rows of synthetic data that accurately reflect the causal structure above. Use the following generation protocol:

1. **Generate root causes**: Sample 'age', 'sex', 'native-country', and 'capital-loss' independently from their marginal distributions in the provided samples.
2. **Propagate causally**: For each subsequent variable, sample its value based ONLY on its direct parents in the graph. Use conditional distributions learned from the data. For example:
   - 'education-num' depends on 'age'
   - 'marital-status' depends on 'age' and 'relationship'
   - 'hours-per-week' depends on 'age' (not on 'Income')
   - 'relationship' depends on 'sex'
   - 'Income' depends on 'marital-status' and 'capital-loss'
   - 'education' depends on 'education-num'
   - 'occupation' depends on 'education', 'Income', and 'native-country'
   - 'capital-gain' and 'workclass' and 'race' depend only on 'Income'
3. **Verify each row**: Check that for every parent-child pair, the generated value is plausible. Key checks:
   - Older ages → higher 'education-num' (e.g., age 50+ typically has education-num ≥ 10)
   - Older ages → more likely married or widowed in 'marital-status'
   - Younger ages → fewer hours in 'hours-per-week' (e.g., < 30 for age < 20)
   - 'relationship' aligns with 'sex' (e.g., 'Husband' for male, 'Wife' for female)
   - 'Income' balanced so that 'capital-loss' > 0 reduces 'Income' > 50K probability
   - 'occupation' differs by 'native-country' (e.g., US-born more likely 'Exec-managerial')
4. **Resample if inconsistent**: If any check fails, discard the entire row and regenerate.

3. **Explicit verification**: After generating each row, verify that for every causal edge (A→B), the value of B is consistent with A given the conditional relationship observed in the data. 2. **Ordered conditional generation** (generate variables strictly in this order, using only their direct parents):
   - First generation layer (children of age, sex, native-country):
     - 'capital-loss': conditioned on 'age' (older ages have higher capital loss probability) and 'sex' (males have slightly higher capital loss)

     - 'occupation': conditioned on 'sex', 'education', 'native-country'

   - Third generation layer (Income and its children):
     - 'Income': conditioned on 'marital-status', 'capital-loss', 'race' (married have higher income; high capital loss reduces income; certain races have lower average income)
     - 'occupation': also conditioned on 'Income' (high-income occupations: Exec-managerial, Prof-specialty; low-income: Other-service, Handlers-cleaners)

   - Hours-per-week: U-shaped distribution—young adults work fewer hours, middle-aged work most, elderly reduce hours; Income >50K must have hours-per-week ≥ 30
   - Race distribution: White ~85%, Black ~10%, others ~5%; this should be reflected in synthetic data

   - Is age consistent with education-num? (e.g., age 18 cannot have Doctorate)
   - Is workclass consistent with occupation? (e.g., occupation 'Farming-fishing' must have workclass 'Self-emp-not-inc' or 'Self-emp-inc' or 'Private'; 'Armed-Forces' must be 'Without-pay' or 'Federal-gov')

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the provided samples. To achieve this, follow this generation protocol:

1. **Root cause sampling**: Generate root causes 'age', 'sex', 'race', and 'capital-loss', and 'native-country' independently based on their marginal distributions from the samples. Note: 'native-country' has no children in this structure.

2. **Conditional generation**: For each non-root variable, sample its value conditioned on its direct causes (parents) in the causal graph, using conditional distributions learned from the data. Do not use non-parent variables. Order generation to respect parent-child dependencies: first roots; then 'education-num' (parent: age); then 'hours-per-week' (parent: age); then 'relationship' (parent: sex); then 'marital-status' (parents: age, relationship); then 'education' (parent: education-num); then 'occupation' (initially from parent: education, but will be overwritten by Income); then 'Income' (parents: marital-status, education-num, capital-loss, race); then 'occupation' (regenerated from parent: Income, conditioned on education); then 'capital-gain' (parent: Income); then 'workclass' (parent: Income).

The final occupation must reflect both education and Income, but ensure causal direction: Income → occupation is the primary driver, with education as a secondary influence.*

3. **Implicit consistency checks**: After generating each row, ensure causal coherence without exhaustive rule lists. The following patterns should hold for the majority of rows:

   - ('education-num' → 'education'): education level must perfectly correspond to education-num (e.g., education-num=9 → 'HS-grad', education-num=10 → 'Some-college', education-num=13 → 'Bachelors', education-num=14 → 'Masters', education-num=16 → 'Doctorate').

   - ('Income' → 'workclass'): 'Self-emp-inc' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Never-worked' should only appear with Income <=50K.
   - Age-Income coherence: No age <18 with Income >50K, and probability of >50K for age 18-25 should be <15%.
   - Education-Income coherence: No education-num=4 (preschool) with Income >50K.

4. **Resampling for realism**: If any row contains a combination that is statistically implausible (e.g., age 18 with Income >50K, or 'Husband' with 'Never-married', or education='Doctorate' with education-num=10, or capital-loss=99999 with Income >50K), discard the row and resample. Resample up to 3 times per row; if after 3 attempts the row is still invalid, generate a new root set and repeat. Use your judgment to avoid obvious contradictions and extreme outliers.

   - `education`, `Income`, `sex` → `occupation`: High education (Bachelors+) with high Income → 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty'; low education with low Income → 'Craft-repair', 'Machine-op-inspct'; Female → more 'Adm-clerical', 'Sales'; Male → more 'Craft-repair', 'Exec-managerial'; native-country no longer a parent, but `sex` is.
   - `Income` → `capital-gain`: Income ≤50K typically has capital-gain = 0; rarely up to 99999; Income >50K often has capital-gain > 0, up to 99999.
   - `Income` → `capital-loss`: Income ≤50K typically has capital-loss = 0; Income >50K may have non-zero loss (since they have capital to lose). Example: For >50K, allow occasional capital-loss up to 4356.
   - `Income` → `workclass`: Income ≤50K → 'Private', 'Self-emp-not-inc', 'State-gov' common; Income >50K → 'Self-emp-inc', 'Federal-gov', 'Private'.

4. **Resample if inconsistent**: If any check fails, discard the entire row (not just the problematic variable) and regenerate the root causes for a new row.

5. **Output format**: Exactly 10 rows in valid JSON array, each with keys in this exact order: age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income. Ensure all values are realistic and consistent with real-world patterns from the provided samples. Output only the JSON array, no other text.
</Task>

"Based on analysis of the 47.62% and 50.0% scores, I identified that overly complex structures with redundant edges (like `Income → hours-per-week` in the 45.0% version) create contradictions, while the 50.0% version's simplification (`education-num → Income`, `occupation → Income`) still missed key causal realism and lacked explicit root-cause structure for `capital-loss`. The 47.62% version performed better due to stronger root-cause independence but suffered from over-constraint.

I will adopt a structure that:
- Keeps `capital-loss` as a root cause (not caused by `age`/`sex`) to avoid unnecessary complexity, as it performed well in 47.62%.
- Removes `Income → hours-per-week` (redundant with `age → hours-per-week` and contradictory to real-world patterns where hours cause income, not vice versa).
- Adds `race → Income` (critical for demographic realism, as seen in 45.0% improvements).
- Adds `education-num → Income` (direct education effect, improving causal realism).
- Removes `occupation → Income` (since `Income → occupation` is more causally defensible in this dataset—occupation choice is influenced by income potential).
- Keeps `Income → capital-gain`, `Income → workclass` as children of `Income`.
- Provides clear generation order and avoids exhaustive rule lists, allowing the model flexibility.

</Causal structure>

1. **Root cause sampling**: Generate root causes 'age', 'sex', 'race', 'capital-loss', and 'native-country' independently based on their marginal distributions from the samples. Note: 'native-country' has no children in this structure.

2. **Conditional generation**: For each non-root variable, sample its value conditioned on its direct causes (parents) in the causal graph, using conditional distributions learned from the data. Do not use non-parent variables.    *Note: Generation order must be: roots → education-num → hours-per-week → relationship → marital-status → education → occupation (from education) → Income → occupation (from Income) → capital-gain → workclass. The final occupation should reflect both its parents: education and Income.*

3. **Implicit consistency checks**: After generating each row, ensure causal coherence without exhaustive rule lists. The following patterns should hold for the majority of rows:
   - ('age' → 'education-num'): older ages should correlate with higher education-num.
   - ('age' → 'hours-per-week'): working-age adults (25-65) should have higher hours; younger (<25) and older (>65) should have lower.
   - ('age' → 'marital-status'): younger ages should be more likely 'Never-married'; older ages more likely 'Married-civ-spouse'.
   - ('sex' → 'relationship'): Males should be more likely 'Husband'; females more likely 'Wife' or 'Not-in-family'.
   - ('marital-status' → 'Income'): Married individuals should have higher Income >50K probability than never-married.
   - ('education-num' → 'education'): education level should perfectly correspond to education-num (e.g., education-num=10 → 'Some-college', education-num=14 → 'Bachelors').
   - ('education-num' → 'Income'): Higher education-num should increase the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('education' → 'occupation'): 'Prof-specialty' should require high education (e.g., 'Doctorate', 'Masters'); 'Other-service' should match low education.
   - ('Income' → 'occupation'): 'Exec-managerial' and 'Prof-specialty' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Handlers-cleaners' and 'Other-service' for Income ≤50K.
   - ('capital-loss' → 'Income'): High capital loss (>1000) should reduce the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('race' → 'Income'): 'White' should have a higher Income >50K probability than 'Black' or 'Other'.
   - ('Income' → 'capital-gain'): Income >50K should be strongly associated with positive capital gain; Income ≤50K should have capital-gain = 0 in most cases.
   - ('Income' → 'workclass'): 'Self-emp-inc' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Never-worked' should only appear with Income ≤50K.

4. **Resampling for realism**: If any row contains a combination that is statistically implausible (e.g., age 18 with Income >50K, or 'Husband' with 'Never-married', or education='Doctorate' with education-num=10), discard the row and resample. Use your judgment to avoid obvious contradictions.

5. **Output format**: Provide exactly 10 rows of synthetic data, each row containing all features in the exact order: ['age', 'workclass', 'education', 'education-num', 'marital-status', 'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', 'hours-per-week', 'native-country', 'Income']. Order generation to respect parent-child dependencies: first roots; then 'education-num' (parent: age); then 'hours-per-week' (parent: age); then 'relationship' (parent: sex); then 'marital-status' (parents: age, relationship); then 'education' (parent: education-num); then 'occupation' (parent: education, with additional influence from Income—generate later); then 'Income' (parents: marital-status, education-num, capital-loss, race); then 'occupation' (parent: Income—overwrite the previously generated occupation based on Income and education); then 'capital-gain' (parent: Income); then 'workclass' (parent: Income).  
   *Note: Generation order must be: roots → education-num → hours-per-week → relationship → marital-status → education → occupation (from education) → Income → occupation (from Income) → capital-gain → workclass. The final occupation should reflect both its parents: education and Income.*

3. **Implicit consistency checks**: After generating each row, ensure causal coherence without exhaustive rule lists. The following patterns should hold for the majority of rows:
   - ('age' → 'education-num'): older ages should correlate with higher education-num.
   - ('age' → 'hours-per-week'): working-age adults (25-65) should have higher hours; younger (<25) and older (>65) should have lower.
   - ('age' → 'marital-status'): younger ages should be more likely 'Never-married'; older ages more likely 'Married-civ-spouse'.
   - ('sex' → 'relationship'): Males should be more likely 'Husband'; females more likely 'Wife' or 'Not-in-family'.
   - ('marital-status' → 'Income'): Married individuals should have higher Income >50K probability than never-married.
   - ('education-num' → 'education'): education level should perfectly correspond to education-num (e.g., education-num=10 → 'Some-college', education-num=14 → 'Bachelors').
   - ('education-num' → 'Income'): Higher education-num should increase the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('education' → 'occupation'): 'Prof-specialty' should require high education (e.g., 'Doctorate', 'Masters'); 'Other-service' should match low education.
   - ('Income' → 'occupation'): 'Exec-managerial' and 'Prof-specialty' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Handlers-cleaners' and 'Other-service' for Income ≤50K.
   - ('capital-loss' → 'Income'): High capital loss (>1000) should reduce the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('race' → 'Income'): 'White' should have a higher Income >50K probability than 'Black' or 'Other'.
   - ('Income' → 'capital-gain'): Income >50K should be strongly associated with positive capital gain; Income ≤50K should have capital-gain = 0 in most cases.
   - ('Income' → 'workclass'): 'Self-emp-inc' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Never-worked' should only appear with Income ≤50K.

4. **Resampling for realism**: If any row contains a combination that is statistically implausible (e.g., age 18 with Income >50K, or 'Husband' with 'Never-married', or education='Doctorate' with education-num=10), discard the row and resample. Use your judgment to avoid obvious contradictions.

5. **Output format**: Provide exactly 10 rows of synthetic data, each row containing all features in the exact order: ['age', 'workclass', 'education', 'education-num', 'marital-status', 'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', 'hours-per-week', 'native-country', 'Income']. Output each row as a valid JSON object, with the entire output being a valid JSON array of 10 such objects. Each row must be a complete, consistent realization of the causal graph.</Task>"

"Based on analysis of the 47.62% and 50.0% scores, I identified that overly complex structures with redundant edges (like `Income → hours-per-week` in the 45.0% version) create contradictions, while the 50.0% version's simplification (`education-num → Income`, `occupation → Income`) still missed key causal realism and lacked explicit root-cause structure for `capital-loss`. The 47.62% version performed better due to stronger root-cause independence but suffered from over-constraint.

I will adopt a structure that:
- Keeps `capital-loss` as a root cause (not caused by `age`/`sex`) to avoid unnecessary complexity, as it performed well in 47.62%.
- Removes `Income → hours-per-week` (redundant with `age → hours-per-week` and contradictory to real-world patterns where hours cause income, not vice versa).
- Adds `race → Income` (critical for demographic realism, as seen in 45.0% improvements).
- Adds `education-num → Income` (direct education effect, improving causal realism).
- Removes `occupation → Income` (since `Income → occupation` is more causally defensible in this dataset—occupation choice is influenced by income potential).
- Keeps `Income → capital-gain`, `Income → workclass` as children of `Income`.
- Provides clear generation order and avoids exhaustive rule lists, allowing the model flexibility.

[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('sex', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'marital-status'), ('marital-status', 'Income'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education-num', 'Income'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('Income', 'occupation'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('race', 'Income')]
</Causal structure>

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the provided samples. To achieve this, follow this generation protocol:

1. **Root cause sampling**: Generate root causes 'age', 'sex', 'race', 'capital-loss', and 'native-country' independently based on their marginal distributions from the samples. Note: 'native-country' has no children in this structure.

2. **Conditional generation**: For each non-root variable, sample its value conditioned on its direct causes (parents) in the causal graph, using conditional distributions learned from the data. Do not use non-parent variables. Order generation to respect parent-child dependencies: first roots; then 'education-num' (parent: age); then 'hours-per-week' (parent: age); then 'relationship' (parent: sex); then 'marital-status' (parents: age, relationship); then 'education' (parent: education-num); then 'occupation' (parent: education, with additional influence from Income—generate later); then 'Income' (parents: marital-status, education-num, capital-loss, race); then 'occupation' (parent: Income—overwrite the previously generated occupation based on Income and education); then 'capital-gain' (parent: Income); then 'workclass' (parent: Income).  
   *Note: Generation order must be: roots → education-num → hours-per-week → relationship → marital-status → education → occupation (from education) → Income → occupation (from Income) → capital-gain → workclass. The final occupation should reflect both its parents: education and Income.*

3. **Implicit consistency checks**: After generating each row, ensure causal coherence without exhaustive rule lists. The following patterns should hold for the majority of rows:
   - ('age' → 'education-num'): older ages should correlate with higher education-num.
   - ('age' → 'hours-per-week'): working-age adults (25-65) should have higher hours; younger (<25) and older (>65) should have lower.
   - ('age' → 'marital-status'): younger ages should be more likely 'Never-married'; older ages more likely 'Married-civ-spouse'.
   - ('sex' → 'relationship'): Males should be more likely 'Husband'; females more likely 'Wife' or 'Not-in-family'.
   - ('marital-status' → 'Income'): Married individuals should have higher Income >50K probability than never-married.
   - ('education-num' → 'education'): education level should perfectly correspond to education-num (e.g., education-num=10 → 'Some-college', education-num=14 → 'Bachelors').
   - ('education-num' → 'Income'): Higher education-num should increase the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('education' → 'occupation'): 'Prof-specialty' should require high education (e.g., 'Doctorate', 'Masters'); 'Other-service' should match low education.
   - ('Income' → 'occupation'): 'Exec-managerial' and 'Prof-specialty' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Handlers-cleaners' and 'Other-service' for Income ≤50K.
   - ('capital-loss' → 'Income'): High capital loss (>1000) should reduce the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('race' → 'Income'): 'White' should have a higher Income >50K probability than 'Black' or 'Other'.
   - ('Income' → 'capital-gain'): Income >50K should be strongly associated with positive capital gain; Income ≤50K should have capital-gain = 0 in most cases.
   - ('Income' → 'workclass'): 'Self-emp-inc' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Never-worked' should only appear with Income ≤50K.

4. **Resampling for realism**: If any row contains a combination that is statistically implausible (e.g., age 18 with Income >50K, or 'Husband' with 'Never-married', or education='Doctorate' with education-num=10), discard the row and resample. Use your judgment to avoid obvious contradictions.

5. **Output format**: Provide exactly 10 rows of synthetic data, each row containing all features in the exact order: ['age', 'workclass', 'education', 'education-num', 'marital-status', 'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', 'hours-per-week', 'native-country', 'Income']. </Causal structure>

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the provided samples. To achieve this, follow this generation protocol:

1. **Root cause sampling**: Generate root causes 'age', 'sex', 'race', 'capital-loss', and 'native-country' independently based on their marginal distributions from the samples. Note: 'native-country' has no children in this structure.

2. **Conditional generation**: For each non-root variable, sample its value conditioned on its direct causes (parents) in the causal graph, using conditional distributions learned from the data. Do not use non-parent variables. Order generation to respect parent-child dependencies: first roots; then 'education-num' (parent: age); then 'hours-per-week' (parent: age); then 'relationship' (parent: sex); then 'marital-status' (parents: age, relationship); then 'education' (parent: education-num); then 'occupation' (parent: education, with additional influence from Income—generate later); then 'Income' (parents: marital-status, education-num, capital-loss, race); then 'occupation' (parent: Income—overwrite the previously generated occupation based on Income and education); then 'capital-gain' (parent: Income); then 'workclass' (parent: Income).  
   *Note: Generation order must be: roots → education-num → hours-per-week → relationship → marital-status → education → occupation (from education) → Income → occupation (from Income) → capital-gain → workclass. The final occupation should reflect both its parents: education and Income.*

3. **Implicit consistency checks**: After generating each row, ensure causal coherence without exhaustive rule lists. The following patterns should hold for the majority of rows:
   - ('age' → 'education-num'): older ages should correlate with higher education-num.
   - ('age' → 'hours-per-week'): working-age adults (25-65) should have higher hours; younger (<25) and older (>65) should have lower.
   - ('age' → 'marital-status'): younger ages should be more likely 'Never-married'; older ages more likely 'Married-civ-spouse'.
   - ('sex' → 'relationship'): Males should be more likely 'Husband'; females more likely 'Wife' or 'Not-in-family'.
   - ('marital-status' → 'Income'): Married individuals should have higher Income >50K probability than never-married.
   - ('education-num' → 'education'): education level should perfectly correspond to education-num (e.g., education-num=10 → 'Some-college', education-num=14 → 'Bachelors').
   - ('education-num' → 'Income'): Higher education-num should increase the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('education' → 'occupation'): 'Prof-specialty' should require high education (e.g., 'Doctorate', 'Masters'); 'Other-service' should match low education.
   - ('Income' → 'occupation'): 'Exec-managerial' and 'Prof-specialty' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Handlers-cleaners' and 'Other-service' for Income ≤50K.
   - ('capital-loss' → 'Income'): High capital loss (>1000) should reduce the probability of Income >50K.
   - ('race' → 'Income'): 'White' should have a higher Income >50K probability than 'Black' or 'Other'.
   - ('Income' → 'capital-gain'): Income >50K should be strongly associated with positive capital gain; Income ≤50K should have capital-gain = 0 in most cases.
   - ('Income' → 'workclass'): 'Self-emp-inc' should be more common for Income >50K; 'Never-worked' should only appear with Income ≤50K.

4. **Resampling for realism**: If any row contains a combination that is statistically implausible (e.g., age 18 with Income >50K, or 'Husband' with 'Never-married', or education='Doctorate' with education-num=10), discard the row and resample. Use your judgment to avoid obvious contradictions.

5. **Output format**: Provide exactly 10 rows of synthetic data, each row containing all features in the exact order: ['age', 'workclass', 'education', 'education-num', 'marital-status', 'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', 'hours-per-week', 'native-country', 'Income'].
